=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a simple connected undirected graph with N vertices and M edges, where the vertices are numbered 1 to N and the edges are numbered 1 to M. Edge i connects vertex u_i and vertex v_i in both directions.
Initially, there is a piece A on vertex S and a piece B on vertex T. Here, S and T are given as input.
You may perform the following operation any number of times in any order:

- Choose either piece A or piece B, and move it from its current vertex to an adjacent vertex via an edge. However, you cannot make a move that results in both pieces ending up on the same vertex.

Your goal is to reach the state in which piece A is on vertex T and piece B is on vertex S.
Determine whether this is possible, and if it is, find the minimum number of operations required to achieve it.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M S T
u_1 v_1
u_2 v_2
\vdots
u_M v_M

Output

If it is impossible to achieve the goal, print -1.
If it is possible, print the minimum number of operations required.

Constraints


- 2 \le N \le 2\times 10^5
- \displaystyle N-1 \le M \le \min\left(\frac{N(N-1)}{2},\,2\times 10^5\right)
- 1 \le u_i < v_i \le N
- The given graph is simple and connected.
- 1 \le S, T \le N
- S \neq T
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4 4 3 4
2 4
1 4
3 4
2 3

Sample Output 1

3

For example, the following sequence of operations completes the goal in three moves:

- Move piece A to vertex 2.
- Piece A is on vertex 2, piece B is on vertex 4.


- Move piece B to vertex 3.
- Piece A is on vertex 2, piece B is on vertex 3.


- Move piece A to vertex 4.
- Piece A is on vertex 4, piece B is on vertex 3.



It is impossible to complete the goal in fewer than three moves, so print 3.

Sample Input 2

2 1 1 2
1 2

Sample Output 2

-1

No matter how you move the pieces, you cannot achieve the goal.

Sample Input 3

5 6 3 5
1 2
2 3
1 5
2 4
1 3
2 5

Sample Output 3

4


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's characterize.

Given two walks P = p_0=S,...,p_a=T and Q = q_0=T,...,q_b=S (B actual). We need assign move times. Since moves alternate? We can schedule all moves of one token in chunks. A sufficient and maybe necessary condition: There exists an index i along P and j along Q such that before time, A traverses prefix p_0..p_i, B traverses suffix? Let's think.

We can view each token's progress along its walk as a nondecreasing integer (number of moves made by that token). State at any time is (p_{i}, q_{j}) where i = number of A moves so far, j = number of B moves so far, with i from 0..a, j from 0..b. A move increments i by1, B move increments j by1. The sequence of states is a monotone path in grid from (0,0) to (a,b), moving right/up, avoiding cells where p_i = q_j. The total moves a+b. We need a monotone path in the a×b grid from (0,0) to (a,b) avoiding "bad" cells (i,j) with p_i=q_j. Moves right/up. This is key! We don't need alternate in arbitrary order; any interleaving corresponds to a monotone path. The initial (0,0): p_0=S, q_0=T distinct. Goal (a,b): p_a=T, q_b=S distinct. Need avoid bad cells.

Thus the problem reduces to: Choose walks P from S to T and Q from T to S (lengths a,b) such that there exists a monotone path from (0,0) to (a,b) in the grid avoiding diagonal equality p_i=q_j. Total a+b minimized. Since P and Q can be any walks. But perhaps we can choose P and Q as shortest paths plus detours.

A monotone path avoiding bad cells exists iff there is no "barrier" of bad cells separating start from goal? In grid, bad cells are positions where the two tokens would be at same vertex at same progress count. Since P and Q are walks, bad cells form some set.

If P and Q are internally vertex-disjoint (no p_i=q_j except endpoints? Actually endpoints p_0=S=q_0? Wait q_0=T, not S. The reverse of Q is S-T path R: r_j = q_{b-j}. Bad p_i = q_j means A at p_i, B at q_j. If P and R are internally vertex-disjoint, then p_i != q_j for all i,j except possibly p_a=T=q_0 and p_0=S=q_b. These are bad? p_a=T, q_0=T: if i=a,j=0, that's initial? Actually initial i=0,j=0, not i=a,j=0. The cell (a,0) would mean A finished at T while B hasn't moved (still T), collision; but monotone path need not visit (a,0) unless it moves all A first. We can avoid. Similarly (0,b). Internal disjoint ensures no bad cells except maybe endpoints with mismatched indices. A monotone path can avoid them by not going to corners. So schedule exists.

If P and Q share a vertex x at positions i0 and j0, then cell (i0,j0) bad. But monotone path can go around if there is another route. A single bad cell not a barrier. Example tree with leaf: P (A) = S=1 ->2->4->2->3 (length4), Q (B)=3->2->1 (length2). Let's compute bad cells. P positions: p0=1,p1=2,p2=4,p3=2,p4=3. Q: q0=3,q1=2,q2=1. Bad: p1=2=q1 (i=1,j=1); p3=2=q1 (i=3,j=1). Also p0=1=q2 (i=0,j=2) initial? A at S, B at S after B finished; cell (0,2) bad but can avoid by moving A before B finishes? In our schedule, B finished at S while A at4 (i=2,j=2), not (0,2). p4=3=q0 (i=4,j=0) bad. Need monotone path from (0,0) to (4,2) avoiding these. Is there one? Schedule sequence: A,A,B,B,A,A corresponds i,j: (0,0)->(1,0)->(2,0)->(2,1)->(2,2)->(3,2)->(4,2). Bad cells: (2,1)? p2=4,q1=2 ok; (2,2) p2=4,q2=1 ok; (3,2) p3=2,q2=1 ok; (4,0) avoided. So yes.

So the grid perspective is useful. We need choose P,Q and monotone path. But maybe we can combine P and Q into a single walk? The monotone path through grid corresponds to an ordering of moves; at each step, either advance P or Q. The actual sequence of positions is a "shuffle" of P and Q. Collision avoidance means when advancing P to p_i, q_j != p_i; when advancing Q to q_j, p_i != q_j. This is exactly the original.

Wait path with leaf: shortest closed walk containing S,T and not backtracking? Let's compute shortest "cycle" in the graph after adding a new edge? This is the shortest closed walk starting and ending at S that visits T and is not the simple path backtrack? It is exactly the shortest "closed walk containing S and T that is not the path twice" because to visit T and return to S in a tree, you must traverse the S-T path twice, and if you want to "swap" maybe also traverse a side branch twice. The shortest closed walk from S that visits T and returns to S is 2D=4 (go to T and back). But invalid. The next shortest closed walk that visits T and returns to S and has an "extra" edge is 2D+2 (go to x, branch, back, then to T, back). Let's examine if any solution yields a closed walk W = P + Q (A path S->T then B path T->S). This W starts/ends at S and visits T. Its length is total moves. Conversely, given any closed walk W from S to S that visits T, can we split it into P (first part S->T) and Q (rest T->S) and schedule? Not always; if W is just go to T and back along same path, split P=shortest path, Q=reverse path, no schedule. But maybe if W has no "immediate backtracking"? It traverses edge 2-3 twice (A to T, B back), edge 2-4 twice (detour), edge 1-2 twice. It is an Eulerian trail of a tree doubled plus extra branch? It is a closed walk with all edges even. The invalid W=1-2-3-2-1 also all edges even. Difference: invalid W is the unique path doubled; valid W has a "spur" at x (edge 2-4 doubled). So W must contain a cycle in the multigraph sense? In graph theory, a closed walk decomposes into cycles; a tree doubled has no cycle, but W with spur is still a tree doubled (all edges even) and no cycle in underlying graph. It has a vertex of degree 4 in the multigraph (2 appears four times). So not simple.

Let's analyze the case where the shortest path P between S and T is unique. Let P = v_0=S, v_1, ..., v_D=T. So need allow walks from v_i to v_i that are not empty; minimal length 2 if there is any neighbor off P. So "alternative route" between v_i and v_i of length 2 (go to neighbor and back) suffices. This is not a simple cycle but a closed walk.

Thus passing opportunity exists if there is a vertex x on P (possibly endpoint?) with an off-path neighbor (side branch) and x internal? For endpoint S, an off-path neighbor alone did not allow pass because route from S to S via neighbor length2; but we found it didn't work. Wait branch at S is a route from S to S of length2 (S-neighbor-S). It did not allow pass. Why? Because if A leaves S to neighbor, B can move to S, but A cannot return to S (occupied) nor reach T without passing through S? Actually A at neighbor, to reach T must go back to S, but B at S. So route S->neighbor->S doesn't help because it returns to S, which is behind B. To get to the other side of B, A needs to re-enter P at a vertex ahead of B (i.e., not S). Thus a route from v_i to v_i only helps if v_i is not an endpoint? If v_i=S, returning to S doesn't advance; if v_i=T, leaving T and returning to T doesn't help B get past. So side branch at endpoint is useless. A cycle connecting S to internal v_i helps because re-enters ahead. A cycle connecting T to internal helps. A cycle connecting two internal vertices helps. So re-entering at same x works because x is between S and T; B has passed x.

Therefore, the "overtaking" can be modeled as choosing a vertex/route that allows one token to move from one side of the other to the other side. In terms of the unique shortest path P, the tokens need to exchange order along P. This is possible iff there exists a "bypass" connecting two positions a < b on P (with 0 <= a < b <= D, and not both endpoints? Actually if a=b internal, bypass is side branch at x; if a<b, bypass is an off-P path between v_a and v_b). The token can use this bypass to move from position a to position b (or b to a) without traversing the P segment between them, while the other token traverses that segment. The cost extra is length of bypass - (b-a) if a<b; if a=b, bypass length 2r? But for side branch at x, bypass from x to x via off-path has length 2 (or more), and the P segment length 0, so extra = 2. More generally, for any off-P walk from v_a to v_b, the extra over using P segment is L - (b-a) if a<b, or L + (a-b) if a>b? Since P is unique shortest, any off-P path between v_a and v_b has length >= |a-b|. If a<b, extra = L - (b-a). If a=b, extra = L (closed walk length) (since P segment length0), minimal 2 if there is an off-P neighbor. If a>b, the bypass goes backward; to use it to pass, one token would move from a to b (backward) while the other moves forward; extra = L - (b-a) = L + (a-b). This is at least 2(a-b) and likely not minimal compared to a forward bypass. But maybe a backward bypass could allow pass? Example cycle connecting v_2 to v_1 (backward) could be used by A to move from v_2 to v_1? That would move A backward, not helpful to get ahead. To pass, one token needs to move from left of the other to right; using a backward bypass might move right token to left? Let's think. If A is left of B initially. To swap, A must end right of B. A can use a bypass from v_a to v_b with a<b to jump forward. B can use the reverse. A backward bypass from v_b to v_a would move A backward, opposite. So only a<b (or a=b internal) matters. However, if we consider B moving from T to S, B uses bypass backward from v_b to v_a; that's equivalent to A using forward from v_a to v_b. So yes.

Thus minimal extra likely:
- If there are at least two shortest paths (i.e., some off-P path between S and T of length D), answer = 2D.
- Else, let P be the unique shortest path. Now, how to compute this minimum efficiently?

This is reminiscent of finding the shortest cycle in a graph with vertex potentials, specifically the minimum "reduced cost" path between vertices on a distinguished path. We can model each edge (x,y) with reduced cost w(x,y) = 1 + distS[x] - distS[y] (if moving x->y). This is nonnegative because distS[y] <= distS[x]+1. For any path, reduced cost = len + distS[start] - distS[end]. We need the minimum reduced cost of a path whose endpoints are P vertices, not the P segment, with constraints.

Observation: For any edge (x,y), w(x,y) + w(y,x) = 2. The P edges forward have w=0, backward w=2. Off-P edges have w maybe 0,1,2? Since distS can differ by at most1. If edge connects vertices at same distS, w=1 in both directions. If connects distS increases by1, forward w=0, backward w=2. If connects distS decreases by1, forward w=2, backward w=0. So reduced costs are 0,1,2. The P edges are exactly some zero-cost edges along shortest path DAG? Actually any edge that goes from distS d to d+1 has w=0 in that direction. There may be many zero-cost edges not on P (they are edges on shortest paths from S). If there are multiple shortest paths, there is a zero-cost path from S to T not P, giving c=0. If unique, the zero-cost edges form a tree? Let's see.

We can think of the graph directed by increasing distS. A shortest path from S to T follows zero-reduced-cost edges. If unique, there is exactly one directed zero-cost path from S to T. Any other zero-cost path would imply multiple shortest paths. So the zero-cost directed graph (edges from d to d+1) has no alternative S-T path, but may have branches that lead to dead ends (vertices not on P) with no path to T? Actually if a zero-cost edge goes to an off-P vertex x with distS=d+1, and x cannot reach T with zero cost, then it's a dead-end branch. Such branches are off-P but at same distance levels; they can serve as spurs? If x is adjacent back to P, then a path P->x->P forms a cycle with reduced cost? Let's compute: P vertex v_d -> x (distS d+1) w=0; x -> v_d (back) w=2; total closed walk reduced cost2, spur at v_d. If x connects to v_{d+1} (another P vertex), then path v_d -> x -> v_{d+1} has reduced cost 0 + (1 + distS[x] - distS[v_{d+1}]) = 0 + (1 + d+1 - (d+1)) =1? Wait x distS=d+1, edge x-v_{d+1} also increases? distS[v_{d+1}]=d+1, same level, w=1. Total reduced cost1. This corresponds to off-P path length2 between v_d and v_{d+1}, cost=2-1=1. Good.

So c is the minimum reduced cost of a path in the directed graph (with weights w) from a P vertex to another P vertex (or same internal) that is not the zero-cost P path. Since weights nonnegative and P path has cost0. We need the shortest "non-tree" path between P vertices in this directed acyclic? The graph with weights w may have cycles (undirected edges give positive cycles). But w is nonnegative, and zero-cost edges go from distS d to d+1. There are no zero-cost cycles except? An edge between same dist has w=1, backward w=2. So zero-cost edges form a DAG by distS. We can compute the minimum reduced cost path from any P vertex to any P vertex (with start index <= end) excluding the P path. This is like finding the shortest path in the graph with weights w between any two vertices on P, but the P path itself has cost0; we need the next shortest. Since weights are nonnegative and P path cost0, the minimum positive cost path between P vertices can be found by considering edges not on P and shortest paths with reduced costs.

Maybe we can compute c using Dijkstra on a contracted graph? N,M 2e5, D up to N. We need O(M log N) perhaps.

Let's define h(v)=distS[v]. For any path from x to y, reduced cost = len + h(x)-h(y). We want min over paths between P vertices u=v_a, v=v_b with a<=b (and if a=b internal) of reduced cost, excluding the P segment. Let's examine the structure of zero-reduced-cost edges. An edge (x,y) has w(x,y)=0 iff distS[y] = distS[x]+1. The intersection of reachable from S and can reach T is exactly the set of vertices on shortest S-T paths; if unique, just P. A path between P vertices with reduced cost c can be decomposed into zero-cost segments (following shortest path DAG) and one or more "costly" edges. Since we want minimum c, perhaps c is the minimum over edges (x,y) not on P of something like distS? Let's derive.

Let's design multi-source Dijkstra:

- sources: for i, v = P[i], push (i, v, i) meaning distance = i, source_id = i (or source vertex P[i]). Note source_id should be the index i, not vertex id, because initial distance depends on index. If two P vertices? unique indices. Off-P vertices have no source label initially.
- For each vertex, maintain best1_dist, best1_src, best2_dist, best2_src. Initialize INF.
- When relaxing a label (d, src) to neighbor y with nd=d+1, we need add if src is new. The function add_label(y, nd, src):
  - if src == best1_src: if nd < best1_dist update best1; but since Dijkstra, nd won't be smaller than existing if already finalized? Could be if we process labels in increasing order, first label for a given source is shortest. We can simplify: when we pop a label, it's the shortest distance for that source to that vertex? In multi-source Dijkstra, if we allow multiple labels per vertex, we can just push all relaxations and skip if the label is not among the best two for that vertex. Standard: maintain best two labels; when adding, if src same as best1, update if nd<best1; else if src same as best2, update; else if nd < best1, shift best1 to best2, set best1; else if nd < best2, set best2. Then push if label was accepted.
- Since edge weights are positive, Dijkstra works. Number of labels per vertex limited to 2, so heap size O(2M) maybe.

But there is a subtlety: We need the second-best label from a *different source* at each P vertex. The trivial source is the vertex itself with distance index. Since we initialize all P vertices as sources, at P vertex v_j, best1 will be source j distance j. But what if another source has distance smaller than j? Is that possible? But for off-P vertices, best1/best2 from P sources will be computed. We don't need labels at off-P vertices except to propagate. The second-best at P vertices may come from a path that goes through another P vertex as intermediate. Is that allowed? H has no P edges, but a path from source v_i to target v_j could pass through another P vertex v_k. That would mean the off-P path from v_i to v_j via v_k; the segment from v_i to v_k is an off-P path, and from v_k to v_j is off-P. The reduced cost from v_i to v_j = (len1+len2)+i-j = (len1+i-k) + (len2+k-j) = cost1 + cost2. This is sum of two off-P excursion costs. The minimum valid bypass would be just one of the segments, with smaller cost. I think standard best-two different-source Dijkstra is safe for computing the minimum distance from any source to any vertex with a different source, because for any path from source s to target t, if at an intermediate vertex v a label from t is shorter than from s, then the path s->v->t is not needed: either the segment from v to t is a better path from some source to t, or the cycle at t is shorter. For minimum over all targets, the segment will be considered. This is intuitive.

This is exactly the reduced cost of the segment from a to b (if a<b) or b to a. The total reduced cost of the optimal s-t path is the sum of reduced costs of these segments? Thus we can compute c_alt using only the closest-source Dijkstra (one label per vertex), not two labels. This is simpler and efficient.

Let's detail:

- Build H = graph without P edges.
- Run multi-source Dijkstra on H with sources = P vertices v_i, initial distance = i. Edge weights 1. For each vertex x, store d[x] = minimum over sources s of (dist_H(s,x) + idx(s)), and src[x] = the source index achieving it.
- Then for each undirected edge (u,v) in H:
  if src[u] != src[v]:
      a = src[u]; b = src[v]
      cand = d[u] + d[v] + 1 - 2 * max(a, b)
      c_alt = min(c_alt, cand)
- Also internal spur cost: for each internal P vertex v_i (0<i<D), if it has any incident edge in H (i.e., an edge to a vertex not on P? Let's search memory: To find shortest cycle in an undirected graph, run BFS from each vertex? No. For "shortest cycle through a set" or "second shortest path", one can run multi-source Dijkstra and for each edge with different nearest sources, update dist[u]+dist[v]+w. However, our initial distances are not uniform; but the same principle should hold. To be extra robust, we can use the best-two labels approach, which is also not too hard and avoids tie concerns. Let's evaluate best-two Dijkstra correctness for our exact objective. We need min over paths between P vertices of dist + start_idx - end_idx. If we run multi-source Dijkstra with initial distance idx and keep best two labels from distinct sources at each vertex, then at each P vertex v_j, the second-best label distance minus j gives the minimum over paths from a different source to v_j of dist + start_idx - j. This directly computes the desired c_alt (including paths that may pass through other P vertices, but min okay). It doesn't rely on edge partition or tie. It also naturally handles all orientations because the second-best at the smaller-index endpoint will be the smaller value. This seems more straightforward and robust, at cost of storing two labels per vertex and propagating more labels. Complexity O(2M log(2M)) ~ 4e5 log, fine in Python if optimized.

Let's consider if best-two Dijkstra might miss a path due to discarding a label at an intermediate vertex that later yields the minimum at a target, as discussed. The standard best-two labels from distinct sources is used to compute for each vertex the shortest path from any source excluding its own source; if a label is discarded at an intermediate vertex because it's not among the best two from distinct sources, could it be needed to reach a target? Suppose at vertex x, labels from sources s1,s2 are best. A label from s3 is discarded. For any target t, continuing from x with s3 gives distance d3 + dist(x,t). Continuing from s1 or s2 gives <= d3 + dist(x,t) (since d1,d2 <= d3). The source of the continuing label might equal t, causing invalid same-source at target. If the best label at x is from source t (the target), then continuing to t gives a closed walk, invalid. But the discarded s3 label might be needed to reach t. Could this happen while d_t <= d_s3 at x? Then the closed walk t->x->t has length 2*dist(t,x) <= d_s3 + dist(t,x) = d_s3 + dist(x,t). The valid path s3->x->t length = d_s3 + dist(x,t). The closed walk is shorter or equal. If t is internal, spur cost2 <= closed walk length <= valid path, so answer <=2, so the valid path cannot be the minimum if it's >2. If valid path cost could be 1? A path from s3 to t via x with cost1 would be a direct edge, no intermediate. So if valid path cost >=2, spur cost2 <= it. Thus not needed. If t is endpoint, closed walk invalid, but if d_t <= d_s3 at x, then there is an off-P path from endpoint t to x. This path itself is a valid bypass from x (if x is P? x may be off-P) to t? If x is off-P, the path from the last P vertex before x to t is valid and has cost <= closed walk? Let's reason similarly: If target endpoint T has a shorter path to intermediate x than source s3, then the path from x to T is a valid bypass from some P vertex to T with cost <= d_t? Actually the path T->x has length L. The forward bypass from the P vertex on that path nearest T? The reduced cost of T->x as a backward path is L + D - distS[x]? Not sure. But I suspect the minimum valid bypass will be found by the segment from the last P vertex to T, which has source not T at that P vertex? I think best-two is safe and directly computes c_alt. Let's implement best-two to avoid edge partition tie concerns. But best-two Dijkstra with two labels per vertex can be a bit more code; still manageable.

Let's outline best-two Dijkstra:

- INF = 10**18
- best1_d = [INF]*N, best1_src = [-1]*N
- best2_d = [INF]*N, best2_src = [-1]*N
- heap = []
- For each i, v in enumerate(P):
    add_label(v, i, i)  # distance i, source index i
- add_label function:
    if src == best1_src[v]:
        if d < best1_d[v]:
            best1_d[v] = d; heappush
        return
    if src == best2_src[v]:
        if d < best2_d[v]:
            best2_d[v] = d; heappush
        return
    if d < best1_d[v]:
        best2_d = best1_d, best2_src = best1_src
        best1_d = d, best1_src = src
        heappush
    elif d < best2_d[v]:
        best2_d = d, best2_src = src
        heappush
- Dijkstra pop (d, u, src):
    # Need verify this label is still one of the best two for u with this src.
    if src == best1_src[u]:
        if d != best1_d[u]: continue
    elif src == best2_src[u]:
        if d != best2_d[u]: continue
    else:
        continue
    for v in adj[u]:
        if is_P_edge(u,v): continue
        add_label(v, d+1, src)

Since we push labels when accepted, and may update best1 to best2, old heap entries for the shifted label remain; the check handles.

At the end, for each P vertex v_j:
    # best1 should be source j with distance j.
    # But due to Dijkstra, maybe best1 could be another source with distance < j? We argued impossible. But if it happens (shouldn't), then the best1 is actually a valid path from another source with negative reduced cost? impossible. So best1 is trivial.
    if best2_src[v_j] != -1:
        c_alt = min(c_alt, best2_d[v_j] - j)
But wait, best2_d[v_j] is the minimum distance from a source different from best1_src. If best1_src is not j (shouldn't), then best2 might be j or another. We should compute for each P vertex the best label whose source != j, not necessarily best2 if best1 is not j. To be safe:
    For v_j, find the best label among best1/best2 with src != j. Since best1 likely src=j, best2 is the one. If best1 src != j, then best1 is a valid alternative; but impossible. We can write:
        alt_d = INF
        if best1_src[v] != j: alt_d = min(alt_d, best1_d[v])
        if best2_src[v] != j: alt_d = min(alt_d, best2_d[v])
        if alt_d < INF: c_alt = min(c_alt, alt_d - j)
This is robust.

But wait, best2_d[v_j] - j gives the reduced cost of a path from some source i to v_j. If i > j, this value is larger than the reverse path's reduced cost. The reverse path will be considered at v_i as best2 from source j, giving smaller value. So the global min over all P vertices will be the forward orientation. Good.

But if neighbor is another P vertex non-consecutive, impossible. So check if degree in H >0. We can compute by iterating adjacency of P[i] and skipping P edges; if any, spur=True. Then c = min(c, 2).

The cost of the whole is sum of costs of segments. The minimum will be a segment with smaller cost. So considering simple off-P paths between P vertices with no internal P vertices is enough; Dijkstra over H allows passing through P vertices, but min will pick segment. Good.

Now, let's think about the multi-source Dijkstra initial distances. We set source v_i distance i = distS[v_i]. For an off-P vertex x, d[x] = min_i (dist_H(v_i,x)+i). This is the minimum over paths from a P vertex to x of ordinary length + index. The reduced cost of a path from v_i to v_j is d? At target v_j, best2 from different source gives min_i (dist_H(v_i,v_j)+i) - j. This is exactly c_alt. Good.

But what about paths in H that start at v_i and end at v_j but use P vertices as intermediate; dist_H is ordinary distance in H, so yes.

Now, internal spur cost 2: If an internal P vertex has an off-P neighbor, the path v_i -> neighbor -> v_i has length2 in H, same source. The reduced cost for a walk from S to T using this spur is 2. But does the multi-source Dijkstra best2 at v_i capture it? No, same source. We add separately. If the off-P neighbor is not directly adjacent but there is an off-P path from v_i to v_i of length >2, there must be a first off-P neighbor, so spur cost2 is available. Thus min with 2 is enough.

Now, what if the only valid bypass is a spur at an internal vertex, but that internal vertex is not on the unique shortest path? It must be on P to allow pass. If off-P branch attaches to an off-P vertex that attaches to P at internal vertex, the spur is at the P vertex, cost2. Good.

Now, answer could be 2D + 1, 2D + 2, etc. Is 2D + c always the minimum? Need lower bound: Any solution yields an S-T walk for A and T-S walk for B. Since P is unique shortest, at least one token must deviate from P. The first time the two tokens pass each other corresponds to an off-P path between two P vertices (or internal spur) with reduced cost c. The total extra over 2D is at least c. We won't prove in code.

Let's consider if multiple shortest paths answer 2D always, even if D=1? Multiple shortest paths impossible for D=1 because simple graph no parallel edges. So if count > D+1, D>=2. Good.

Now, let's think about BFS distances. N up to 2e5, M up to2e5. Use deque.

Implementation details for performance:

- Use sys.stdin.buffer.read().split() to parse ints.
- Build adjacency as list of lists. For 2e5 edges, list of lists okay. Use 0-index.
- BFS function:
  from collections import deque
  def bfs(start):
      dist=[-1]*N
      dist[start]=0
      q=deque([start])
      while q:
          u=q.popleft()
          for v in adj[u]:
              if dist[v]==-1:
                  dist[v]=dist[u]+1
                  q.append(v)
      return dist
- D = distS[T].
- Count = sum(1 for i in range(N) if distS[i]+distT[i]==D)
- If count > D+1: print(2*D); return. Note D+1 int.
- Reconstruct P:
  P_rev=[]
  cur=T
  while cur!=S:
      P_rev.append(cur)
      # find predecessor
      nxt=-1
      for prev in adj[cur]:
          if distS[prev] == distS[cur]-1:
              nxt=prev
              break
      # In unique shortest path, nxt should not be -1. If multiple, choose first? But count>D+1 would have returned, so unique.
      cur=nxt
  P_rev.append(S)
  P = P_rev[::-1]
  # D should equal len(P)-1
  pos=[-1]*N
  for i,v in enumerate(P): pos[v]=i
- is_P_edge(u,v): if pos[u]!=-1 and pos[v]!=-1 and abs(pos[u]-pos[v])==1. Since graph simple and no nonconsecutive P-P edges, this is sufficient. But what if there is an edge between P[i] and P[i+1] that is not the unique shortest path edge? In a simple graph, there is exactly one edge; it's the P edge. Good.
- Dijkstra:
  INF=10**18
  best1_d=[INF]*N; best1_src=[-1]*N
  best2_d=[INF]*N; best2_src=[-1]*N
  heap=[]
  def add_label(v, d, src):
      # We'll inline for speed? Function call inside loop may be okay but can optimize.
  For each i,v in enumerate(P):
      add_label(v, i, i)
  While heap:
      d,u,src = heappop(heap)
      # validate
      if src == best1_src[u]:
          if d != best1_d[u]: continue
      elif src == best2_src[u]:
          if d != best2_d[u]: continue
      else:
          continue
      for v in adj[u]:
          if pos[u]!=-1 and pos[v]!=-1 and abs(pos[u]-pos[v])==1:
              continue
          nd = d+1
          # add_label(v, nd, src)
          # inline:
          if src == best1_src[v]:
              if nd < best1_d[v]:
                  best1_d[v]=nd
                  heappush(heap,(nd,v,src))
          elif src == best2_src[v]:
              if nd < best2_d[v]:
                  best2_d[v]=nd
                  heappush(heap,(nd,v,src))
          else:
              if nd < best1_d[v]:
                  best2_d[v]=best1_d[v]; best2_src[v]=best1_src[v]
                  best1_d[v]=nd; best1_src[v]=src
                  heappush(heap,(nd,v,src))
              elif nd < best2_d[v]:
                  best2_d[v]=nd; best2_src[v]=src
                  heappush(heap,(nd,v,src))
  Note: If nd == best1_d[v] with different src, we might want to keep it as best2 if best2 worse. The condition nd < best2_d[v] will handle if nd < best2. If nd == best2, no need. If nd == best1 and best2 INF, then nd < best2, so we set best2 to same distance different source. Good. If nd > best1 but < best2, set. If nd == best1 and best2 also same? no need.
  However, if src == best1_src[v] and nd < best1_d[v], update. Since Dijkstra, nd won't be less than existing if already finalized, but okay.
- After Dijkstra, compute c_alt:
  c_alt=INF
  for j,v in enumerate(P):
      alt_d=INF
      if best1_src[v] != j:
          alt_d = min(alt_d, best1_d[v])
      if best2_src[v] != j:
          alt_d = min(alt_d, best2_d[v])
      if alt_d < INF:
          c_alt = min(c_alt, alt_d - j)
  Note: alt_d - j could be 0? If best1_src[v] != j and best1_d[v] == j, that would mean another source reaches v with distance j, reduced cost0, implying multiple shortest paths; not in unique case. But if it happens, c_alt=0 and answer=2D, but we would have returned earlier. So okay.
- Internal spur:
  c_spur=INF
  for i in range(1,D):
      v=P[i]
      for to in adj[v]:
          if pos[to]!=-1 and abs(pos[to]-i)==1:
              continue
          # found off-P edge
          c_spur=2
          break
      if c_spur==2: break
- c = min(c_alt, c_spur)
- if c==INF: print(-1) else print(2*D + c)

Wait, in Dijkstra, we skip P edges. But what about an off-P edge between two P vertices that are not consecutive? We said impossible in unique shortest path. But if due to distS, an edge between v_i and v_j with |i-j|>1 would imply distS[v_j] <= i+1 < j, contradiction. So none. If an edge between v_i and v_j with |i-j|=1 is the P edge. So skip condition correct.

Now, let's test Dijkstra on a graph where H has no edges but internal spur? If H has no edges, no internal spur. c INF -> -1. Good.

Let's test a graph where unique shortest path and an off-P edge between internal P vertex and endpoint? That's a P-P edge nonconsecutive? Example P=1-2-3-4, edge 2-4 (v1 to v3). This would imply distS[4] <= distS[2]+1 =2, but D=3, contradiction. So impossible. Edge 1-3 would imply distS[3]<=1, contradiction. So no.

Now, let's consider if count > D+1 but D=1? Count can't >2 because only S,T have sum1? If there is another vertex x with distS[x]+distT[x]=1, then distS[x]=0 or1, distT[x]=1 or0. If distS[x]=0 => x=S; distT[x]=1. If distS[x]=1, distT[x]=0 => x=T. No other. So count=2. Good.

Now, let's think about memory. best arrays of size N with Python ints: 4 arrays ~ 4*2e5*28 bytes ~22MB, plus adj ~ maybe 30MB, okay. Heap size O(M) ~2e5 tuples of 3 ints, maybe large but okay? Each tuple ~64 bytes, 2e5 ~12MB. Total maybe under 256MB? AtCoder Python memory often 1024MB, okay. We can optimize by using lists of ints.

Could we avoid Dijkstra if c_alt can be computed by BFS? Edge weights 1, initial distances not uniform. Dijkstra with heap is fine.

But note: The graph H is unweighted, but initial distances differ. We can use a priority queue. Since edge weights are 1 and initial distances up to N, we could use Dijkstra with heap. O(M log N).

Potential issue: The multi-source Dijkstra with best-two labels may push many labels. Each vertex can accept up to 2 labels, but when best1 shifts to best2, the old best1 label remains in heap and will be skipped. Number of accepted labels per vertex <=2? Actually a vertex can accept a label that becomes best2, then later another label shifts it out? Let's see: We only keep best two. A label can be accepted as best2, then later if a new best1 arrives, the old best1 becomes best2, and the previous best2 is discarded. The discarded label was already in heap and will be skipped. A vertex can accept more than 2 labels over time? Suppose best1=A, best2=B. New C < best1: best2 becomes A (old best1), B discarded. Later D < best1? best1 is C, best2 A. New E < best1: best2 becomes C, A discarded. So each time a new label is accepted, one label may be discarded. A vertex can accept many labels over time? It can accept a new best1 many times, each time discarding current best2 and shifting old best1. But can there be many decreasing labels for the same vertex? Dijkstra pops in increasing distance, so a later label has distance >= previous popped? Actually a label with smaller distance would be popped earlier. For a given vertex, the first accepted label is the smallest distance (from some source). Since Dijkstra processes in increasing distance, no later label can be smaller than the first. So best1 never changes to a smaller distance after first? Wait initial sources at P vertices have distances equal to index; an off-P vertex may first receive a label from a nearby source with distance d. Later, could another source provide a smaller distance? Dijkstra pops in increasing distance, so if a label with smaller distance existed, it would have been popped earlier and accepted. Thus the first accepted label for a vertex is the global minimum d. best1_d never decreases after first. best2 can be updated to smaller? It can be set when a second source label arrives; later another second source with smaller distance than current best2 but larger than best1 could update best2. This can happen multiple times? Since distances increase, best2 can decrease? Wait later labels have larger or equal distance, so best2 cannot decrease after it's set? It might be set to a label with distance d2, then a later label with distance d3 < d2? Not possible because Dijkstra pops in increasing order; d3 would have been popped before d2. So best2 is set once? Let's think: A vertex may receive labels in increasing distance order. The first label sets best1. The second distinct-source label sets best2 (if distance >= best1). Any later label has distance >= second, so it won't improve best2. Thus each vertex accepts at most 2 labels! Is that true? What about a label with same source but shorter distance? Dijkstra order prevents shorter later. So yes, each vertex accepts at most 2 labels. Therefore heap size O(N+M) actually O(N) accepted, but we push each accepted label; at most 2N pushes, plus initial D pushes. So heap size O(N). Good. We don't need to worry about many labels. The validation skip handles old shifted labels? If best1 shifts? But best1 never shifts to a smaller distance; however, when a second label arrives with distance equal to best1? It becomes best2, no shift. If a label with same distance but different source arrives before best2 set, it sets best2. No shift. So old labels not invalid except if we update best1 for same source? Not needed. But keep validation.

Wait, initial sources: For a P vertex v_j, the first label is from itself distance j. Later, another source label with distance >j arrives, sets best2. No shift. For an off-P vertex, first label from closest source sets best1; second distinct source sets best2. No further updates. So each vertex exactly two labels. Good.

But is it possible that the first label for an off-P vertex is from source A, and later a label from source B with smaller distance than A? No, Dijkstra order. So best1 fixed. Thus the algorithm is essentially multi-source Dijkstra with two nearest sources; correct.

Now, let's consider if there are multiple shortest paths but count > D+1, answer 2D. Need ensure 2D moves can be scheduled. We choose two distinct shortest paths P and Q. Let A follow P, B follow reverse Q. Since both length D. We need monotone path avoiding collisions. Earlier we argued bad cells only on anti-diagonal i+j=D at shared vertices; distinct paths give a gap. But what if P and Q share all vertices except one but the gap cell is blocked by off-anti-diagonal bad cells? We argued no off-anti-diagonal because both shortest paths reach shared vertices at same distance. Let's formalize: P_i and Q_j (where Q is S-T path, B actual reverse). Bad cell (i, j) if P_i = Q_{D-j}? Wait B actual q_j = Q_{D-j}. If P and Q are both shortest from S to T, any shared vertex x has distS[x]=position in P = position in Q. Thus P_i = Q_k implies i=k. Bad cell (i, D-i) on anti-diagonal. Distinct P,Q means there is some d with P_d != Q_d, so cell (d, D-d) is not bad. A monotone path from (0,0) to (D,D) can pass through (d, D-d) if it is unblocked. Let's scrutinize the best-two Dijkstra correctness with initial distances not zero. Suppose we have a path from source a to target b with reduced cost C. In Dijkstra, the label from a will propagate along the path with distance = dist + a. At target b, if this label is not among best two because there are two other sources with smaller distances at b, then C is not the minimum for b. But could it be the global minimum for some other target? The path ends at b, so no. If the label is discarded at an intermediate vertex, we argued the remaining path to b would not yield the global minimum. I think okay.

Let's try to construct a counterexample for best-two Dijkstra with nonzero initial distances. We need a valid path from source s to target t with reduced cost C that is the global minimum, but at some intermediate vertex x, the label from s is not among best two because two other sources have smaller d at x, and continuing from those sources to t gives invalid or larger reduced cost. If two other sources have smaller d at x, then their paths to x are shorter (ordinary distance + index). Continuing from x to t gives ordinary distance d_other + dist(x,t). The reduced cost to t from that other source = d_other + dist(x,t) + idx(other) - idx(t). We need compare to C = (d_s + dist(x,t)) + idx(s) - idx(t) where d_s = dist(s,x)+idx(s). Since d_other < d_s, the continued path from other has smaller ordinary distance but maybe larger index. The reduced cost difference = (d_other + idx(other)) - (d_s + idx(s))? Wait d_other already includes idx(other). Let's define label value L = dist(source,x)+idx(source). At x, L_other < L_s. Continuing to t adds dist(x,t) to both, and reduced cost to t = L + dist(x,t) - idx(t). The idx(source) is already in L. So if L_other < L_s, the continued path from other to t has smaller reduced cost than from s to t! Because reduced cost = L + dist(x,t) - idx(t), independent of source index except through L. Thus the s->t path cannot be the minimum if at x another source has smaller L. Therefore discarding non-best labels is safe. This is a key insight: We are minimizing L = dist + idx(source), and the reduced cost to target t is L + dist(x,t) - idx(t). If a label at x has smaller L, it dominates for all continuations. The only issue is if the dominating source equals the target t, making the continuation a same-source closed walk. But if source = t and L_t < L_s at x, then reduced cost of continuing from t to t is L_t + dist(x,t) - idx(t) = dist(t,x)+idx(t)+dist(x,t)-idx(t) = 2*dist(t,x). The s->t reduced cost is L_s + dist(x,t) - idx(t). Since L_t < L_s, 2*dist(t,x) < L_s + dist(x,t) - idx(t)? Let's check: L_t = dist(t,x)+idx(t). L_t < L_s implies dist(t,x)+idx(t) < dist(s,x)+idx(s). Then 2*dist(t,x) < dist(s,x)+dist(t,x)+idx(s)-idx(t) = s->t reduced cost? Yes, because idx(t) cancels? Compute s->t cost = dist(s,x)+dist(x,t)+idx(s)-idx(t). Since dist(t,x)=dist(x,t). Inequality L_t < L_s => dist(t,x)+idx(t) < dist(s,x)+idx(s) => 2*dist(t,x) < dist(s,x)+dist(t,x)+idx(s)-idx(t). So the closed walk at t is smaller. If t is internal, spur cost2 <= 2*dist(t,x) <= s->t cost, so s->t not minimum. If t is endpoint, closed walk invalid, but if t endpoint and L_t < L_s at x, then there is an off-P path from t to x. The segment from the last P vertex on that path to t might be a valid bypass with cost <= 2*dist(t,x)? Actually if t endpoint, a closed walk at t is invalid, but the path from t to x itself, when reversed, connects x to t. If x is off-P, the last P vertex before t on that path gives a valid forward bypass to t with cost <= dist(t,x) - (t_idx - last_idx) maybe <= 2*dist(t,x). This would be found by a label from that last P vertex to t. So the global min would be that segment, not s->t. Thus safe. Great.

So best-two Dijkstra is correct.

Potential bug in Dijkstra add_label: When a new label with same source as best1 but smaller distance arrives, we update best1 but do not shift best2. This is correct. But can this happen? If a shorter path from same source is found after a longer one, Dijkstra would normally pop shorter first. However, because we initialize all P vertices and may push a label from source to a vertex, then later find another path from same source with smaller distance? Dijkstra with positive weights ensures first popped label for a given source-vertex pair is shortest. But our heap may contain a label from source to vertex that was accepted as best2, then later a shorter label from same source? Not possible due to Dijkstra order. So we can simplify: if src == best1_src or best2_src, we only need to check equality, not update. But keep update safe.

When a new label with different source has nd == best1_d[v], and best2_d[v] > nd, we set best2 to nd. This means two sources tie for best. Good. If later another label with same distance but different source arrives, nd < best2? If best2 already same distance, nd < best2 false (equal), so not stored. We only need two labels; if there are three sources at same minimum distance, any two suffice for finding min? For edge partition? For best-two direct objective, if target v_j has best1 self distance j, and multiple other sources with same distance j+c, storing one is enough to get c. If the stored one is not the one that yields global min? They all yield same reduced cost at v_j. For propagation, if two sources tie at an intermediate, storing one may miss a path to a target where the other source has smaller index? But the reduced cost from that intermediate to target depends on L = dist + idx(source). If they tie at L, continuing gives same L + dist - idx(target), independent of source index? Wait L includes idx(source). If two sources have same L at x, then continuing to any target t gives same reduced cost L + dist(x,t) - idx(t). So source identity doesn't matter for cost. Thus storing one is fine. If a target t equals one of the sources, the reduced cost formula with source = t would be invalid same-source, but if L_t ties with L_s at x, then the closed walk cost equals the s->t cost? Let's check: L_t = L_s. s->t cost = L_s + dist(x,t) - idx(t). Closed walk t->x->t cost = 2*dist(t,x). Are they equal? L_t = dist(t,x)+idx(t). L_s = L_t. s->t cost = dist(t,x)+idx(t)+dist(x,t)-idx(t)=2*dist(t,x). So equal. If t internal, spur cost2 <= this, so s->t not needed. If t endpoint, invalid but the path from t to x is a backward path; the forward segment from some P vertex to t has cost <=? likely found. So okay.

Now, let's think about if we can compute c_alt using the edge partition method with one label to reduce code. Best-two code is longer but okay. Let's ensure best-two code doesn't have a bug with initial sources and validation.

Detailed best-two Dijkstra code:

```
INF = 10**18
best1_d = [INF]*N
best1_src = [-1]*N
best2_d = [INF]*N
best2_src = [-1]*N
heap = []

def push_label(v, d, src):
    if src == best1_src[v]:
        if d < best1_d[v]:
            best1_d[v] = d
            heapq.heappush(heap, (d, v, src))
        return
    if src == best2_src[v]:
        if d < best2_d[v]:
            best2_d[v] = d
            heapq.heappush(heap, (d, v, src))
        return
    if d < best1_d[v]:
        best2_d[v], best2_src[v] = best1_d[v], best1_src[v]
        best1_d[v], best1_src[v] = d, src
        heapq.heappush(heap, (d, v, src))
    elif d < best2_d[v]:
        best2_d[v], best2_src[v] = d, src
        heapq.heappush(heap, (d, v, src))
```

But if d == best1_d[v] and src different, and d < best2_d[v], the `elif d < best2_d[v]` will set best2. Good. If d == best2_d[v], no push. If d > best2, no.

When shifting best1 to best2, if best1_src[v] == -1 (INF), best2 becomes INF/-1. Then set best1. Fine.

In Dijkstra pop:
```
while heap:
    d,u,src = heapq.heappop(heap)
    if src == best1_src[u]:
        if d != best1_d[u]: continue
    elif src == best2_src[u]:
        if d != best2_d[u]: continue
    else:
        continue
    for v in adj[u]:
        if pos[u] != -1 and pos[v] != -1 and abs(pos[u]-pos[v]) == 1:
            continue
        push_label(v, d+1, src)
```

One subtlety: If a label is accepted as best2, then later a new best1 shifts the old best1 to best2, and the old best2 is discarded. The old best2 label remains in heap and will be skipped because its src no longer matches best2_src or best1_src. Good.

But can a label be accepted as best1, then later a new best1 with smaller distance arrives? Dijkstra order prevents smaller later. However, due to initial sources with different distances, a vertex might receive a label from a source with distance 10, then later a label from another source with distance 5? Dijkstra pops in increasing distance, so distance5 would be popped before distance10. But what if the distance5 path to the vertex has many edges and is popped after distance10 because heap order is by d, not by path length? d = path_length + source_idx. A path with smaller d is popped first. So if there is a label with d=5, it will be popped before d=10. Thus best1 never decreases. Good.

Now, after Dijkstra, compute c_alt:
```
c_alt = INF
for j, v in enumerate(P):
    alt = INF
    if best1_src[v] != j:
        alt = min(alt, best1_d[v])
    if best2_src[v] != j:
        alt = min(alt, best2_d[v])
    if alt < INF:
        c_alt = min(c_alt, alt - j)
```
If best1_src[v] == -1? For P vertices, best1_src should be j. But if N? no. If alt INF, skip.

Now, internal spur:
```
c_spur = INF
for i in range(1, D):
    v = P[i]
    for to in adj[v]:
        if pos[to] != -1 and abs(pos[to] - i) == 1:
            continue
        c_spur = 2
        break
    if c_spur == 2:
        break
```
Wait, if pos[to] != -1 and abs(pos[to]-i)==1, that's a P edge. If pos[to] == -1, off-P. If pos[to] != -1 and abs !=1, impossible but would be an off-P edge between P vertices; should count as off-P? Actually if there is an edge between P[i] and P[j] with |i-j|>1, it's an off-P edge (not P edge) and could provide a bypass; but impossible in unique case. If it somehow exists, the internal spur check would see pos[to]!=-1 and abs!=1, so it would set c_spur=2, but actually the edge is a chord and c_alt would be negative? But impossible. To be safe, for spur we should check if the edge is not the P edge; any other edge incident to internal P vertex allows a spur (move to that neighbor and back), even if neighbor is another P vertex? If neighbor is another P vertex nonconsecutive, moving to it and back is a valid spur? It would use an off-P edge (chord) to another P vertex; the token could move from v_i to v_j and back, cost2, and allow pass? If there is a chord between v_i and v_j, that's a forward bypass with cost 1-(j-i) negative? impossible. But moving to v_j and back while other passes maybe? If v_j is ahead on P, the token would jump ahead and back; not needed. But impossible. Our condition `pos[to] != -1 and abs(pos[to]-i)==1` skip only P edges; if pos[to]!=-1 and abs!=1, it would count as spur. But such edge cannot exist, so no issue.

However, for Dijkstra skip P edges, we skip if both pos != -1 and abs==1. If a nonconsecutive P-P edge existed, Dijkstra would include it, and c_alt might be negative, but impossible.

Now, let's think about if the graph has an edge between P[i] and P[i+1] but due to multiple shortest paths? We returned. Good.

Now, let's consider if the Dijkstra should skip P edges only, not all edges between P vertices? Yes.

Now, let's think about if the graph has an internal P vertex with an off-P neighbor that is also a P vertex? impossible.

Now, let's think about if the graph has a unique shortest path and an off-P path between v_i and v_j with cost c, but the off-P path is not the shortest path in H between v_i and v_j because there is a shorter path that uses P edges? We skip P edges, but a path using P edges plus off-P could be shorter in original; however, its reduced cost decomposes and the off-P segment has smaller/equal cost. Let's test: Suppose off-P path from v_i to v_j is very long, but there is a path v_i -> (P forward to v_k) -> off-P short to v_j. The off-P short segment from v_k to v_j has cost = len_short + k - j. The full path cost = len_short + (k-i)? Wait full original path length = (k-i) + len_short. Reduced cost = (k-i)+len_short + i - j = len_short + k - j = cost of off-P segment. So the full path cost equals the off-P segment cost, which is a valid bypass from v_k to v_j. Dijkstra will find the off-P segment (since it doesn't need the P prefix). Thus skipping P edges is okay.

Now, let's think about if the off-P segment from v_k to v_j itself uses P edges internally? Then decompose further. Ultimately, the minimal cost is a path in H between two P vertices. Good.

Now, let's consider if the Dijkstra best2 at v_j from source v_i could be a path that starts at v_i, goes off-P, re-enters P at v_k, then goes along P? But P edges skipped, so it cannot go along P. If it re-enters P at v_k and then leaves again via off-P, that's allowed in H (P vertices are just vertices, edges off-P). The path in H from v_i to v_j may pass through v_k and continue via off-P edges. Its reduced cost is sum of off-P segment costs. Now, let's consider if the Dijkstra with best2 labels might be too slow due to Python function call `push_label` inside inner loop. We can inline to speed. Let's write a helper function for clarity? M=2e5, function call per edge maybe okay but could be ~4e5 calls, fine. But to be safe, inline the logic in the relaxation loop. However, code readability vs performance. Python 2e5 edges, function call overhead ~0.1s? Actually 4e5 calls okay. But inside Dijkstra, each accepted label relaxes all edges; total relaxations up to 2M ~4e5, function calls 4e5, fine. We can define nested function; it will access lists via closure, maybe slower but acceptable. AtCoder Python 2e5, 4e5 calls okay. But to be safe, inline.

Let's write Dijkstra loop with inline add:

```
while heap:
    d,u,src = heappop(heap)
    if src == best1_src[u]:
        if d != best1_d[u]: continue
    elif src == best2_src[u]:
        if d != best2_d[u]: continue
    else:
        continue
    nd = d + 1
    for v in adj[u]:
        if pos[u] != -1 and pos[v] != -1 and abs(pos[u] - pos[v]) == 1:
            continue
        if src == best1_src[v]:
            if nd < best1_d[v]:
                best1_d[v] = nd
                heappush(heap, (nd, v, src))
        elif src == best2_src[v]:
            if nd < best2_d[v]:
                best2_d[v] = nd
                heappush(heap, (nd, v, src))
        else:
            if nd < best1_d[v]:
                best2_d[v], best2_src[v] = best1_d[v], best1_src[v]
                best1_d[v], best1_src[v] = nd, src
                heappush(heap, (nd, v, src))
            elif nd < best2_d[v]:
                best2_d[v], best2_src[v] = nd, src
                heappush(heap, (nd, v, src))
```

Note: If src == best1_src[v] and nd < best1_d[v], we update best1 but don't shift best2. This could happen if a shorter path from same source found; but if best1_d decreases, the old best1 label in heap is invalid. However, if best1_d decreases, best2 remains. But can best1_d decrease after best2 is set? Dijkstra order says no, but if it does, the old best1 label with larger d will be skipped because d != best1_d. Good.

If src == best2_src[v] and nd < best2_d[v], update best2. Good.

If src is new and nd < best1_d[v], shift best1 to best2. But if best1_src[v] == src? handled. If best1_src[v] == -1, best2 becomes INF/-1.

Now, initial sources: We need to push all P vertices. But if two P vertices have same index? no. We can do:
```
for i, v in enumerate(P):
    best1_d[v] = i
    best1_src[v] = i
    heappush(heap, (i, v, i))
```
Since each P vertex is distinct, no conflict. What if a P vertex is also reached by another source with smaller distance before its own label is popped? We argued impossible. But if it happens, best1_d[v] would be overwritten by the smaller label, and the self label would be skipped. Then at the end, best1_src[v] != j, and alt_d might be self? But self label was pushed with distance j; if overwritten, best2 might not contain self. Could this cause issues? It shouldn't happen in unique case. To be safe, we could use `add_label` for initial sources to handle if a vertex somehow already has a label? But P vertices are distinct and no prior labels. So direct assignment okay.

Wait, what if D=0? S!=T so no.

D=1, count2, but two edges; our code would skip the P edge? We need skip all edges between P[0] and P[1]? Since graph simple, only one. Good.

Now, let's consider if the graph has a unique shortest path and c_alt found, but the answer 2D+c could be achieved by moving A along the bypass and B along P. We described schedule. Need ensure if the bypass starts at v_i and ends at v_j, and B moves along P from T to S while A is off-P, B might pass through v_i before A has left if A's path from S to v_i takes time. We can order: First move A along P from S to v_i. B is at T, so no collision. Then move A into off-P path (one step). Then move B along P from T to S. B passes v_i after A left; passes v_j before A arrives. Then move A along rest of off-P path to v_j and P to T. This works. If the off-P path is just a single edge from v_i to v_j? Then A moves v_i->v_j in one step; B may still be at T or moving. Need coordinate: If v_j != T, B can pass v_j before A arrives. We can move B first? Let's schedule more carefully for a single-edge bypass (but simple graph no edge between nonconsecutive P vertices; off-P path length at least2 if i<j? Could be length1 only if consecutive P vertices, but that's P edge skipped. So off-P path length >=2 for i<j? Actually if i<j and nonconsecutive, length at least2. If i=0,j=1, off-P path length at least2 (triangle). So A takes at least 2 moves off-P, giving time for B to move. If off-P path length 2, A moves v_i->x->v_j. We can move A v_i->x, then B moves from T to S (or at least past v_j), then A x->v_j. Works. If v_j=T, B must leave T before A x->T; B can move while A at x. Good. If v_i=S, A leaves S into off-P; B can move to S after A left. Good. So schedule works.

Now, let's think about if the bypass path has length 1 between v_i and v_j with i<j? That would be an edge between nonconsecutive P vertices, impossible due to distS. So no issue.

Now, let's consider if the graph has a unique shortest path and an internal spur at v_i, schedule: A moves from S to v_i, then A v_i->x, then B moves from T to S along P, then A x->v_i, then A v_i->T. If B passes v_i while A at x; if A returns to v_i after B has passed, okay. If v_i is close to T, B may reach S before A returns; no collision. Good.

If source k > j, the reduced cost at v_j would be dist + k - j, which is larger than the reverse path from v_j to v_k. But if it's stored as best2, it means dist + k - j < c. The reverse path from v_j to v_k has cost dist + j - k = (dist + k - j) - 2(k-j) < c. So there is an even smaller valid bypass from v_j to v_k. Thus global min even smaller. Good.

Graph: P vertices v0 idx0, v1 idx1, v2 idx2. H: v0 - a - v1 (length2), v1 - b - v2 (length2). There is no direct v0-v2. Off-P paths: v0-v1 cost =2+0-1=1; v1-v2 cost=2+1-2=1. c_alt should be1.
Dijkstra:
sources v0 d0, v1 d1, v2 d2.
a: from v0 d1 src0, from v1 d2? v1 initial1 + edge a? Wait a connected to v0 and v1? H edges v0-a, a-v1, v1-b, b-v2. a from v1 d2 src1. best1 at a src0 d1, best2 src1 d2.

One more detail: In Dijkstra, we skip P edges using `pos[u] != -1 and pos[v] != -1 and abs(pos[u] - pos[v]) == 1`. But if u and v are both P vertices and consecutive, this skips the edge. If u and v are both P vertices and nonconsecutive (impossible), it won't skip, and Dijkstra may find a negative reduced cost. But impossible. If we want to be safe, we could skip only if the edge is in the P edge set. But set lookup might be slower. The pos/abs check is fine.

Now, final code:

```
import sys
from collections import deque
import heapq

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it); M = next(it); S = next(it)-1; T = next(it)-1
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    edges = []
    for _ in range(M):
        u = next(it)-1; v = next(it)-1
        adj[u].append(v); adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u,v))
    def bfs(start):
        dist = [-1]*N
        dist[start]=0
        q=deque([start])
        while q:
            u=q.popleft()
            for v in adj[u]:
                if dist[v]==-1:
                    dist[v]=dist[u]+1
                    q.append(v)
        return dist
    distS=bfs(S); distT=bfs(T)
    D=distS[T]
    cnt=0
    for i in range(N):
        if distS[i]+distT[i]==D:
            cnt+=1
    if cnt > D+1:
        print(2*D)
        return
    # reconstruct P
    P_rev=[]
    cur=T
    while cur!=S:
        P_rev.append(cur)
        nxt=-1
        for prv in adj[cur]:
            if distS[prv] == distS[cur]-1:
                nxt=prv
                break
        cur=nxt
    P_rev.append(S)
    P=P_rev[::-1]
    pos=[-1]*N
    for i,v in enumerate(P):
        pos[v]=i
    INF=10**18
    best1_d=[INF]*N; best1_src=[-1]*N
    best2_d=[INF]*N; best2_src=[-1]*N
    heap=[]
    for i,v in enumerate(P):
        best1_d[v]=i
        best1_src[v]=i
        heapq.heappush(heap,(i,v,i))
    while heap:
        d,u,src=heapq.heappop(heap)
        if src == best1_src[u]:
            if d != best1_d[u]:
                continue
        elif src == best2_src[u]:
            if d != best2_d[u]:
                continue
        else:
            continue
        nd=d+1
        pu = pos[u]
        for v in adj[u]:
            pv = pos[v]
            if pu != -1 and pv != -1 and (pu - pv == 1 or pv - pu == 1):
                continue
            if src == best1_src[v]:
                if nd < best1_d[v]:
                    best1_d[v]=nd
                    heapq.heappush(heap,(nd,v,src))
            elif src == best2_src[v]:
                if nd < best2_d[v]:
                    best2_d[v]=nd
                    heapq.heappush(heap,(nd,v,src))
            else:
                if nd < best1_d[v]:
                    best2_d[v], best2_src[v] = best1_d[v], best1_src[v]
                    best1_d[v], best1_src[v] = nd, src
                    heapq.heappush(heap,(nd,v,src))
                elif nd < best2_d[v]:
                    best2_d[v], best2_src[v] = nd, src
                    heapq.heappush(heap,(nd,v,src))
    c_alt=INF
    for j,v in enumerate(P):
        alt=INF
        if best1_src[v] != j:
            alt = min(alt, best1_d[v])
        if best2_src[v] != j:
            alt = min(alt, best2_d[v])
        if alt < INF:
            c_alt = min(c_alt, alt - j)
    c_spur=INF
    for i in range(1, D):
        v=P[i]
        for to in adj[v]:
            pto = pos[to]
            if pto != -1 and (pto - i == 1 or i - pto == 1):
                continue
            c_spur=2
            break
        if c_spur == 2:
            break
    c = min(c_alt, c_spur)
    if c == INF:
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(2*D + c)
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

One more subtlety: In the Dijkstra, when we initialize best1_d[v]=i for P vertices, we don't use the add_label logic, so if a P vertex is also reached by another source with smaller distance before its own label is popped, best1_d might be overwritten by the other source, and the self label would be skipped. We argued impossible in unique case. But what about a P vertex v_i with i>0 and an off-P path from S (source0) to v_i of length i? That would be another shortest path from S to v_i, and extending to T gives another S-T shortest path, so count >D+1. Not in unique. What about an off-P path from a source k>i to v_i with distance i? That would require dist_H >= k-i, so distance at least k-i. For distance i, need k-i <= i => k<=2i. Could happen? Example source v2 idx2 to v1 idx1 via off-P edge length1, distance =1, d =1+2=3 > best1 self d1. So not smaller. In general, for source k>i, dist_H >= k-i, so d >= k-i + k = 2k-i > i. So not smaller. For source k<i, dist_H >= i-k, d >= i-k + k = i. Equality would mean multiple shortest path; otherwise >i. So self is strictly smallest. Good.

However, let's revisit the lower bound and formula one more time with a potential counterexample: Graph with unique shortest path, an off-P path between v_i and v_j with cost c, but the off-P path is "behind" the other token and the schedule we described moves B all the way to S while A is off-P. What if the off-P path starts at v_i and ends at v_j, and B moving along P from T to S passes through v_i before A has left? We move A into off-P first. But if the off-P path's first step from v_i goes to a vertex that is also on P? No, off-P. So A leaves v_i. Good. If the off-P path is just a single edge from v_i to v_j and v_j is before v_i? We orient i<j. Good.

What if the off-P path starts at v_i and the first step goes to a vertex x that is occupied by B? B is at T, not x. B moves later. Good.

What if the off-P path ends at v_j and B is at v_j when A arrives? We move B to S before A arrives. B can traverse P from T to S while A is off-P; it will pass v_j before A arrives. Since A is off-P, no collision. Good.

Thus schedule valid.

Now, let's think about if the graph has a unique shortest path and c_alt found, but the Dijkstra best2 at v_j from source i might be from a path that starts at v_i and ends at v_j, and the path's source i is not the smaller index; min over all P vertices handles.

Ok.

Let's also consider if the graph has a unique shortest path and c_alt INF, but internal spur exists and D=2, answer6. We simulated. Good.

Now, let's think about if the graph has a unique shortest path and c_alt found, but the Dijkstra best2 at v_j from source i might be from a path that starts at v_i and ends at v_j, and the path's source i is not the smaller index; min over all P vertices handles.

Ok.

I will now produce final code.
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
import heapq
from collections import deque


def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return

    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it)
    M = next(it)
    S = next(it) - 1
    T = next(it) - 1

    adj = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    for _ in range(M):
        u = next(it) - 1
        v = next(it) - 1
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)

    def bfs(start):
        dist = [-1] * N
        dist[start] = 0
        q = deque([start])
        while q:
            u = q.popleft()
            for v in adj[u]:
                if dist[v] == -1:
                    dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                    q.append(v)
        return dist

    distS = bfs(S)
    distT = bfs(T)
    D = distS[T]

    # Count vertices lying on some shortest path from S to T.
    cnt = 0
    for i in range(N):
        if distS[i] + distT[i] == D:
            cnt += 1

    # If there are at least two different shortest paths, 2*D is achievable.
    if cnt > D + 1:
        print(2 * D)
        return

    # Reconstruct the unique shortest path.
    rev = []
    cur = T
    while cur != S:
        rev.append(cur)
        nxt = -1
        for prv in adj[cur]:
            if distS[prv] == distS[cur] - 1:
                nxt = prv
                break
        cur = nxt
    rev.append(S)
    P = rev[::-1]

    pos = [-1] * N
    for i, v in enumerate(P):
        pos[v] = i

    INF = 10**18

    # Multi-source Dijkstra on the graph without the shortest-path edges.
    # Source v_i has initial distance i = distS[v_i].
    best1_d = [INF] * N
    best1_src = [-1] * N
    best2_d = [INF] * N
    best2_src = [-1] * N
    heap = []

    for i, v in enumerate(P):
        best1_d[v] = i
        best1_src[v] = i
        heapq.heappush(heap, (i, v, i))

    while heap:
        d, u, src = heapq.heappop(heap)

        if src == best1_src[u]:
            if d != best1_d[u]:
                continue
        elif src == best2_src[u]:
            if d != best2_d[u]:
                continue
        else:
            continue

        nd = d + 1
        pu = pos[u]

        for v in adj[u]:
            pv = pos[v]

            # Skip edges of the unique shortest path.
            if pu != -1 and pv != -1 and (pu - pv == 1 or pv - pu == 1):
                continue

            if src == best1_src[v]:
                if nd < best1_d[v]:
                    best1_d[v] = nd
                    heapq.heappush(heap, (nd, v, src))
            elif src == best2_src[v]:
                if nd < best2_d[v]:
                    best2_d[v] = nd
                    heapq.heappush(heap, (nd, v, src))
            else:
                if nd < best1_d[v]:
                    best2_d[v], best2_src[v] = best1_d[v], best1_src[v]
                    best1_d[v], best1_src[v] = nd, src
                    heapq.heappush(heap, (nd, v, src))
                elif nd < best2_d[v]:
                    best2_d[v], best2_src[v] = nd, src
                    heapq.heappush(heap, (nd, v, src))

    # Best bypass using an off-shortest-path connection between two path vertices.
    c_alt = INF
    for j, v in enumerate(P):
        alt = INF
        if best1_src[v] != j:
            alt = min(alt, best1_d[v])
        if best2_src[v] != j:
            alt = min(alt, best2_d[v])
        if alt < INF:
            c_alt = min(c_alt, alt - j)

    # A side branch at an internal vertex of the shortest path costs 2.
    c_spur = INF
    for i in range(1, D):
        v = P[i]
        for to in adj[v]:
            pto = pos[to]
            if pto != -1 and (pto - i == 1 or i - pto == 1):
                continue
            c_spur = 2
            break
        if c_spur == 2:
            break

    c = min(c_alt, c_spur)

    if c == INF:
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(2 * D + c)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
